# H-003: Does Bajnok's sumset/critical-number theory bound j*(l)?

Source: arXiv literature pass (2026-07-27, L-015) surfaced B. Bajnok, "Open Problems About
Sumsets in Finite Abelian Groups: Minimum Sizes and Critical Numbers" (arXiv:1512.03038v2, 15 May
2017; full text fetched and read in full for this note, all 14 pages, not just the abstract).

## Verdict: does not map. Bajnok's theory does not apply to R_{j,k} as a black box.

This is a real negative result, derived below, not a hedge. The reasoning is structural (a
genuine mismatch in what kind of object is being summed), confirmed by an explicit numerical
check against this project's own verified data.

## Bajnok's definitions (verbatim from the paper, Section 1.1)

For a finite abelian group $G$ of order $n$ and an $m$-subset $A = \{a_1,\ldots,a_m\}$ of $G$,
and a nonnegative integer $h$:

- $h$-fold sumset: $hA = \{\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_i \mid \lambda_i \in \mathbb{N}_0,\ \sum \lambda_i = h\}$
  (repetition of elements of $A$ allowed).
- $h$-fold restricted sumset: $\hat{h}A = \{\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_i \mid \lambda_i \in \{0,1\},\ \sum \lambda_i = h\}$
  (exactly $h$ *distinct* elements of $A$, summed with equal weight 1).
- $h$-fold signed sumset: $h_{\pm}A = \{\sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i a_i \mid \lambda_i \in \mathbb{Z},\ \sum|\lambda_i| = h\}$.

The **critical number** $\chi(G,h)$ (and $\hat\chi$, $\chi_\pm$ analogously) is the least $m$ such
that the corresponding sumset equals *all of* $G$, **for every** $m$-subset $A$ of $G$. This is a
worst-case/extremal quantity: it's a statement quantified over all possible generating sets of a
given size, not about one specific set.

The critical property that makes all three of these objects tractable to the theory in this
survey: **the value contributed by a chosen element $a_i$ to the sum does not depend on which
other elements are also chosen, or on any notion of rank/order among the chosen elements.** $A$ is
a genuine subset of the group, and summation in an abelian group is symmetric; $\lambda_i$ only
records "how many times / with what sign was $a_i$ used," never "in what position."

## R_{j,k}'s actual structure (re-derived and numerically verified here)

From the project's verified primary-source definition (`literature/notes/L-001.md`, Wirsching
1998):
$$R_{j,k} := \Big\{ \sum_{i=0}^{j} 2^{a_i} 3^i \ \Big|\ j+k \ge a_0 > a_1 > \cdots > a_j \ge 0 \Big\}$$

and the computation actually used (`experiments/E-001-jstar-fast`) works with $R_{l-1,j}$ via the
reduction $R_{j-1,j} \bmod 3^l = 2^{j-l} \cdot R_{l-1,j} \bmod 3^l$ for $j\ge l$ (a unit multiple,
doesn't change coverage). $R_{l-1,j}$ sums $l$ terms (index $i=0,\ldots,l-1$), with
$(l-1)+j \ge a_0 > a_1 > \cdots > a_{l-1} \ge 0$, i.e. a **strictly decreasing sequence of $l$
distinct values drawn from $\{0,1,\ldots,l+j-1\}$** (a ground set of size $l+j$).

A strictly decreasing sequence of $l$ values is just an $l$-element subset $S \subseteq
\{0,\ldots,l+j-1\}$ written in decreasing order. So: choose an $l$-subset $S$, sort it
$s_1 > s_2 > \cdots > s_l$, and the value is
$$\sum_{i=1}^{l} 2^{s_i}\, 3^{\,i-1}.$$

**This is the crux of the mismatch.** The weight applied to a chosen value $s_i$ is $3^{i-1}$,
where $i$ is $s_i$'s *rank* (position) within the sorted selection $S$, not a fixed weight tied
to $s_i$ itself, independent of what else is chosen. Change which other elements are in $S$ and
the same value $s$ can end up at a different rank, hence get multiplied by a different power of
3. There is no fixed candidate set $B \subseteq \mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$ (or any group) for which
$R_{l-1,j} \bmod 3^l$ is expressible as $\hat{l}B$ (or $lB$, or $l_\pm B$) in Bajnok's sense,
because $\hat{h}A$'s defining property is exactly that each element's contribution is
context-independent, and here it provably is not.

### Numerical confirmation

Reproduced the known, verified checkpoint value (`README.md`'s pointwise validation, l=2 j=2:
image = {1,2,5,7} mod 9, missing {4,8}) directly from this subset-plus-rank parametrization:

```python
from itertools import combinations
l, j = 2, 2
vals = set()
for S in combinations(range(l + j), l):
    s = sorted(S, reverse=True)
    v = sum((2**a) * (3**i) for i, a in enumerate(s))
    vals.add(v % (3**l))
print(sorted(vals))   # [1, 2, 5, 7]  -- matches exactly
```

This confirms the subset+rank parametrization is the right restatement of $R_{l-1,j}$ (not just a
plausible-looking guess), which makes the structural argument above concrete rather than
hand-wavy: the object this project actually computes really is rank-weighted, checked against
ground truth, not merely "apparently" rank-weighted from staring at the definition.

## Why the critical-number *framing* doesn't transfer either, independent of the sumset-type mismatch

Even setting aside which of $hA/\hat{h}A/h_\pm A$ (if any) might loosely resemble $R_{l-1,j}$,
Bajnok's critical numbers answer a **universally-quantified worst-case question**: the least $m$
such that *every* $m$-subset's sumset covers $G$. This project's $j^*(l)$ answers a
**existence question about one specific, structured, parametrized family**: does *this
particular* rank-weighted construction, as its size parameter $j$ grows, eventually cover
$(\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z})^*$? These are different questions even when the underlying objects
matched (which they don't here): a critical number bounds the worst case over *all* possible
generating sets of a given size, while $j^*(l)$ is about the behavior of one specific,
non-arbitrary sequence of sets as it grows. A critical-number bound, even if it existed for the
right object, would answer "how many elements does *some* set need in the worst case," not "how
many terms does *this* R_{j,k}-shaped construction need."

## What was and wasn't checked

- Read the full paper (all 14 pages, not just the abstract): Sections 1.1 (definitions and the
  three sumset types), 1.2 ($\rho(G,m,h)$, minimum $h$-fold sumset size, Plagne/Kemperman
  theorems), 1.3 (restricted sumsets, Dias Da Silva–Hamidoune / Alon–Nathanson–Ruzsa for primes),
  1.4 (signed sumsets), 1.5 (unrestricted-$h$ restricted sumsets, $\rho^{\hat{}}(G,m,\mathbb{N}_0)$),
  1.6 (critical numbers $\chi$, $\hat\chi$, $\chi_\pm$, including the fully-resolved
  $\chi^{\hat{}}(G,\mathbb{N}_0)$ result, Theorem 19).
- No theorem or conjecture in the paper treats rank/position-weighted sums (every construction in
  the survey is a plain, order-independent sum of chosen group elements, by design, since that's
  what makes them sumsets of a subset of an abelian group at all).
- Did not find, anywhere in the 44-reference bibliography, an obviously relevant title suggesting
  a treatment of position-weighted or "layered" sumsets; not exhaustively checked (that would be
  its own literature pass, out of scope for this note).

## Confidence and what would need a second opinion

The structural argument (rank-dependent weight breaks the fixed-candidate-set requirement) is, I
believe, solid and not a close call: it's a definitional mismatch, not a subtle numerical
coincidence, and the numerical check above confirms the parametrization used to make that argument
is the actually-correct restatement of $R_{l-1,j}$, not a mis-transcription. I'm confident in the
negative verdict as stated: **this specific paper's theorems do not give a bound on $j^*(l)$**.

Where I'd genuinely want a second opinion (per this project's Rule 11b) before spending further
effort in this direction: whether *some other, more specialized* tool in additive combinatorics
(outside this one survey) handles rank/position-weighted or "layered" sumset problems, and whether
the informal resemblance between $j^*(l)$ and a critical number is enough motivation to attempt an
*analogous, bespoke* argument (not applying Bajnok's theorems as a black box, but using similar
proof techniques, e.g. the polynomial method mentioned in Bajnok's Theorem 20 discussion, or
Erdős–Heilbronn-style arguments) directly on $R_{j,k}$'s actual structure. That would be new
research, not literature lookup, and is exactly the kind of judgment call (worth pursuing vs. not)
that benefits from a second, independent view rather than this session's own say-so.

## Recommendation

Do not pursue Bajnok (arXiv:1512.03038) further as a source of a ready-made bound; the mismatch is
structural, not a matter of reading more carefully. If theoretical bounds on $j^*(l)$ remain worth
pursuing (a real open question, not addressed here), the productive next step would be a fresh
search specifically for "rank-weighted sumset," "layered sumset," "positional sumset," or
"weighted subset-sum covering" literature, or an attempt at a from-scratch argument using
polynomial-method techniques directly on $R_{j,k}$'s definition, not a second pass over this
particular survey.

# 2026-07-27 (second attempt): bespoke polynomial-method / Erdős–Heilbronn argument on R_{j,k} directly

Authorized by the researcher to pursue the bespoke path flagged above, not applying Bajnok's
theorems as a black box but attempting a from-scratch argument on $R_{j,k}$'s actual structure
using polynomial-method / Erdős–Heilbronn-style techniques. **Note on what this authorization is
and isn't**: the researcher's go-ahead is permission to spend effort, not a mathematical second
opinion. Per Rule 11b, any positive result derived below still needs independent verification
(different-vendor model or the researcher's own math background) before being trusted; that gap
is not closed by "the researcher said go ahead," and I flag explicitly below where it applies.

## Verdict: blocked, by two independent obstacles, before any real argument gets underway

Checked the specific technical obstacle raised (does the polynomial method work over
$\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$, which is not a field for $l \ge 2$) first, since it looked like the
likely single point of failure. It is a real obstacle, and there is a second, independent one on
top of it.

### Obstacle 1: $\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$ is not an integral domain for $l\ge2$, and the Combinatorial Nullstellensatz's proof needs "a nonzero degree-$d$ polynomial has at most $d$ roots," which is false there

The Combinatorial Nullstellensatz (Alon 1999) is stated for $F$ a field, precisely because its
proof rests on the elementary fact that a nonzero degree-$d$ univariate polynomial over a field
(or any integral domain) has at most $d$ roots. Verified directly (not taken from a secondary
source) that this fails over $\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$ for $l\ge2$, using the simplest possible
test polynomial $f(x)=x^2$ (degree 2):

```
l=1  m=3          roots of x^2 mod m: [0]                                        count=1
l=2  m=9          roots of x^2 mod m: [0, 3, 6]                                  count=3
l=3  m=27         roots of x^2 mod m: [0, 9, 18]                                 count=3
l=4  m=81         roots of x^2 mod m: [0, 9, 18, 27, 36, 45, 54, 63, 72]         count=9
```

At $l=1$ (where $\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{F}_3$ really is a field), the count matches the
field bound exactly (1 root, $\le$ degree 2). At every $l\ge2$ this project actually computes
with, the root count *exceeds* the degree, by a growing margin ($3^{l-1}$ roots for $x^2$
specifically, easy to see directly: $x^2\equiv0\pmod{3^l}$ iff $3^{\lceil l/2\rceil}\mid x$).
This is not a subtle failure mode; it is the textbook consequence of $\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$
having zero divisors ($3\cdot3^{l-1}\equiv0$), and it directly breaks the root-counting step the
whole polynomial-method machinery (CN, and the Erdős–Heilbronn/Dias da Silva–Hamidoune proof that
uses CN as its engine) is built on. A short web search for extensions of CN to non-field settings
(Alon's original paper, Pete L. Clark's "The Combinatorial Nullstellensätze Revisited" survey,
and a 2025 extension to skew polynomial rings over *division* rings/algebras) confirms the
standard theorem's hypothesis is a field; the one non-field extension found (skew polynomial
rings) is for division rings, which still have no zero divisors; $\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$,
$l\ge2$, is not one. I did not find, in this search, any stated extension of CN or
Erdős–Heilbronn to rings with zero divisors of this specific kind ($\mathbb{Z}/p^k\mathbb{Z}$);
absence of a hit in one search session is not proof none exists in the wider literature, and is
flagged as such, not overstated as exhaustive.

**Two ways around obstacle 1, both checked and both dead ends for this problem specifically**:

- *Reduce to $\mathbb{F}_3$*: valid application domain for CN, but the covering question this
  project asks is specifically about resolution mod $3^l$ for growing $l$; collapsing to mod 3
  throws away exactly the fine structure $j^*(l)$ is about. A bound obtained this way would say
  something about coverage mod 3 (trivial: 3 residues), not mod $3^l$.
- *Lift to $\mathbb{Z}_3$, the 3-adic integers*: $\mathbb{Z}_3$ is an integral domain (no zero
  divisors: the root-counting lemma *does* hold there), but (a) it is infinite, so "covering all
  of a finite group" has to be reformulated as a density/measure statement to even make sense,
  and (b) that reformulation is exactly Tao's beta=1 / density-of-Collatz-preimages framing this
  project already has as H-002, not a new angle opened up by this attempt.

### Obstacle 2: independent of the ring, $R_{j,k}$'s rank-dependent weighting is not a symmetric function of the chosen set, which the polynomial-method sumset machinery requires

This is the same structural fact notes/H-003.md's first section already established for
Bajnok's theorems (rank-in-selection determines the weight $3^{\text{rank}}$, not a fixed
per-element weight), but it independently blocks the polynomial-method route too, for a reason
specific to how that method is built, not just imported from the Bajnok section.

The standard Erdős–Heilbronn-via-Combinatorial-Nullstellensatz proof works with a polynomial
$P(x_1,\ldots,x_h) = \prod_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)\cdot Q(x_1+\cdots+x_h)$ (schematically): the Vandermonde
factor enforces *distinctness* of the chosen values, and the rest of the construction is a
function of the *sum* $x_1+\cdots+x_h$, i.e. symmetric in the $x_i$: relabeling which variable
holds which value doesn't change $x_1+\cdots+x_h$. This symmetry is what lets the argument treat
the $x_i$ as unordered set elements at all.

$R_{l-1,j}$'s value, $\sum_{i=1}^{l}2^{s_i}3^{i-1}$ with $s_1>\cdots>s_l}$, is a function of the
*set* $\{s_1,\ldots,s_l\}$ (the rank of each element is determined once the set is fixed, not an
extra free choice), but it is not a function of the form $\sum_i g(s_i)$ for any fixed $g$
applied independently to each element: which power of 3 a given value $s$ contributes depends on
how many of the *other* chosen values exceed it. Relabeling/permuting which "slot" holds which
value changes the sum, unlike a genuine sumset. There is no way to write this as
$Q(x_1+\cdots+x_l)$ for a symmetric $Q$; the natural polynomial encoding of $R_{l-1,j}$'s value
would need $\sum_i 2^{x_i}\,3^{\#\{k : x_k > x_i\}}$, and the exponent $\#\{k:x_k>x_i\}$ is
itself a non-polynomial, order-dependent function of the *other* variables, not something
Combinatorial Nullstellensatz's machinery (which reasons about coefficients of an honest
multivariate polynomial) is set up to touch, independent of which ring the $x_i$ live in.

### A reframing that might be a real, unexplored path (flagged for a second opinion, not pursued further here)

One structural observation worth recording rather than discarding: the ordering constraint
$a_0>a_1>\cdots>a_l$ that defines $R_{j,k}$ lives on the *exponent/index domain*
$\{0,\ldots,l+j-1\}\subset\mathbb{Z}$, which is genuinely ordered (it's a set of integers), before
any reduction mod $3^l$ happens. The obstruction above is about applying the polynomial method
*in the target ring* $\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$; it says nothing about whether a polynomial-method
argument could work *on the source side*, treating the choice of an $l$-subset of
$\{0,\ldots,l+j-1\}$ (an honest subset-choice problem over $\mathbb{Z}$, or over a large enough
prime field via reduction, where CN applies cleanly) and only mapping to
$\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$ at the very end via the fixed evaluation map
$S\mapsto\sum_{s\in S}2^s3^{\mathrm{rank}(s)}$. This would be a genuinely new construction, not an
application of any existing theorem found in this pass, and I have not attempted it (would
require inventing the right auxiliary polynomial, not looking one up); flagging it as the one
concrete idea from this pass that seems worth a second opinion before deciding whether to invest
real effort, per the instruction not to let this session's own confidence be the final word on a
genuine open research question.

## Confidence

High confidence, and independently re-derived/verified (not inherited from the Bajnok section) on
both obstacles: the direct, off-the-shelf application of Combinatorial Nullstellensatz /
Erdős–Heilbronn to $R_{j,k}$ mod $3^l$ is blocked, both by the ring not being an integral domain
for $l\ge2$ (verified computationally above) and by the rank-dependent weighting not being
expressible as a symmetric sumset (re-derived independently of the Bajnok write-up, same
underlying fact, different proof route). This is a second clean negative result, not a hedge.

**What still needs a second opinion, explicitly, before further investment**: the reframing idea
above (source-side polynomial method on the ordered index domain, mapped to
$\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$ only at the evaluation step) is untested and could be either a real
opening or a dead end for reasons not yet visible. This is exactly the kind of hard, open
mathematical judgment call this project's Rule 11b says should get an independent view (a
different-vendor model or the researcher's own background) rather than being settled by this
session on its own authority. The go-ahead already given authorizes spending effort, not this
specific judgment.

## Recommendation

H-003 stays `open-unexplored`, not closed. Two black-box approaches (Bajnok's survey theorems,
vanilla polynomial-method/Erdős–Heilbronn applied directly to the target ring) are now both ruled
out with independently-derived, verified negative results. The one remaining candidate direction
(source-side polynomial method on the ordered index domain) is a from-scratch research idea, not
a literature application, and should get a second opinion before real effort goes into it.

# 2026-07-27 (third attempt): the "ordered exponent domain" reframing, precisely stated

Authorized by the researcher to pursue the one reframing flagged, unattempted, at the end of the
second round above: a polynomial-method argument on the *ordered exponent domain* instead of
inside $\mathbb{Z}/3^l\mathbb{Z}$ directly. **Same standing note as the last two rounds**: the
researcher's go-ahead authorizes spending effort, it is not the independent mathematical second
opinion Rule 11b calls for; any positive claim below still needs that before being trusted. There
is no positive claim below to worry about, but the caveat is restated as instructed regardless.

## Verdict: the reframing does not add real power, because it turns out to be the *same object* as the original definition, not a genuinely different one

Following the instruction to first state the construction precisely enough to attempt a proof,
before attempting anything: what exactly is "the ordered exponent domain"?

Re-reading Wirsching's actual definition (`notes/H-001.md`, `literature/notes/L-001.md`), $R_{j,k}$
was never defined by "choose an unordered subset, then sort it and read off ranks." It is defined
directly as a sum over an **explicit descending chain**:
$$R_{j,k} := \Big\{ \textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{j} 2^{a_i}3^i \ \Big|\ j{+}k \ge a_0 > a_1 > \cdots > a_j
\ge 0 \Big\}.$$
The "choose a set $S$, sort it, and let the value be $\sum 2^{s_i}3^{\mathrm{rank}(s_i)}$"
formulation used in the first (Bajnok) section of this note is a *faithful restatement* of this
same object (confirmed there numerically), not a different, more constrained special case of it.
There is no separate "unordered" version of $R_{j,k}$ that a rank/sorting operation is applied to;
the ordering constraint $a_0>a_1>\cdots>a_j$ **is** the original definition, present from the
start, not introduced as an artifact of translating into Bajnok's sumset language.

Numerically re-confirmed this is the same object either way it's parametrized (direct descending
chain vs. subset-then-sort), reproducing the same known checkpoint value:
```python
from itertools import combinations
l, j = 2, 2
vals = {sum((2**a[i])*(3**i) for i in range(l))
        for a in (sorted(c, reverse=True) for c in combinations(range(l+j), l))}
print(sorted(v % 3**l for v in vals))   # [1, 2, 5, 7] -- matches, as expected (same object)
```

**Why this sinks the specific reframing being tested**: the Combinatorial-Nullstellensatz /
Erdős–Heilbronn machinery's actual value-add is *breaking symmetry* on an otherwise unordered,
symmetric sumset: the Vandermonde factor $\prod_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)$ exists specifically to force
*distinctness* among variables that would otherwise be freely interchangeable, which is what lets
the argument treat "which values got chosen" as the only thing that matters (order-independent).
$R_{j,k}$'s domain has no such symmetry to begin with: it is *already* an explicitly ordered chain
by definition, with no relabeling freedom (the constraint isn't merely $a_i$ pairwise distinct, it
is a specific total order, and each position's exponent-of-3 weight is fixed by that position, not
discovered after the fact by sorting). There is no symmetric object here for a Vandermonde-style
device to act on; the "reframing" just re-derives the same explicit map
$f(a_0,\ldots,a_{l-1}) = \sum_i 2^{a_i}3^i \bmod 3^l$ on the same explicit domain
$\{(a_0,\ldots,a_{l-1}) : l{+}j{-}1\ge a_0>\cdots>a_{l-1}\ge0\}$ that the project already computes
directly. Working "on the source side" doesn't introduce a new polynomial-method entry point,
because there was never an unordered/symmetric structure on the source side to exploit in the
first place; $j^*(l)$ is, and always was, a direct question about one explicit, already-ordered
map's image, not a sumset needing symmetry-breaking to become tractable.

A secondary, independent reason CN-style tools don't gain traction even granting the reframing:
CN's coefficient-extraction step is a *field*-polynomial argument, and $f$'s term $2^{a_i}$ is
exponential, not polynomial, in the variable $a_i$; making that rigorous inside CN's framework
would require its own nontrivial construction that was not attempted here (a genuine, separate,
unresolved technical gap, flagged rather than glossed over), but the point above (no symmetry to
break) already makes the reframing not useful even if this second issue were somehow solved.

## A genuinely different, more promising lead surfaced by the required prior-art search (not pursued, flagged only)

Searching for prior art on "weighted/positional subset sums, prime-power modulus" (as instructed)
surfaced a different technique family that looks like a much better fit than the polynomial method,
worth recording even though not pursued here: **character sums / Ramanujan sums**, the classical
analytic-number-theory tool for exact or asymptotic subset-sum counting in $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$
for *any* $n$, prime or composite. Concretely: J. Li and D. Wan, "Counting subset sums of finite
abelian groups" (fetched and read the introduction and Theorem 1.1/Corollary 1.2 directly,
`https://www.math.uci.edu/~dwan/liwan4.pdf`) give an **exact formula**, via Ramanujan's
trigonometric sums and the Möbius function, for $N(k,b)$, the number of $k$-element subsets of a
finite abelian group $A$ summing to a target $b\in A$, valid for cyclic $\mathbb{Z}_n$ of any
modulus $n$ (prime or prime-power, no field requirement; Ramanujan/Gauss sums are defined via
roots of unity, not via the root-counting lemma that sank both prior attempts). This is exactly
the kind of "does a structured sum hit every residue mod $3^l$" machinery that's native to
composite moduli, unlike Combinatorial Nullstellensatz.

**This is not a ready-made answer**: Li–Wan's theorem is for *plain, unweighted* $k$-subset sums
of the *whole* group (every chosen element contributes itself, order-independent), not for
$R_{j,k}$'s rank-weighted sum over a restricted domain: the same core mismatch (position-dependent
weight $3^{\mathrm{rank}}$) that blocked both prior approaches would need to be addressed before
this technique family applies directly. But character-sum methods are built to handle exponential
sums over $\mathbb{Z}/p^k\mathbb{Z}$ as a matter of course (this is their entire domain of
applicability in analytic number theory), which is precisely where both previous attempts failed
structurally. Adapting a character-sum/exponential-sum argument to $R_{j,k}$'s actual rank-weighted
structure is a real, unexplored, and plausible-looking direction, genuinely new research, not a
literature lookup, and, per the same standing instruction as the last two rounds, a judgment call
that should get a second opinion before real effort goes in, not this session's own say-so.

## Confidence

High confidence in the negative verdict on the *specific* reframing tested (source-side polynomial
method on the ordered domain): it is not a subtle miss, it is a category error (there is no
symmetric structure on that domain for the polynomial method's central device, Vandermonde-based
symmetry-breaking, to act on); the domain was never unordered in the first place. Lower confidence
is appropriate for the character-sum lead: it was found via a single search session, read only at
the introduction/main-theorem level for one paper (Li–Wan), and its applicability to $R_{j,k}$'s
specific rank-weighted structure is not yet worked out at all.

## Recommendation

All three candidate approaches attempted or considered so far under H-003 (Bajnok's survey,
direct polynomial-method/Erdős–Heilbronn, and this ordered-domain reframing) are now ruled out
with derived, checked negative results. H-003 stays `open-unexplored`. If further effort goes into
a theoretical bound on $j^*(l)$, the productive next candidate is character-sum / exponential-sum
methods (Ramanujan sums, Gauss sums, or Weyl-sum-style estimates) applied to $R_{j,k}$'s actual
rank-weighted structure directly, rather than another attempt at fitting it into an existing
unweighted-sumset framework. This is a new, unstarted research direction, not a continuation of
what's been tried, and should get an independent opinion before investment, per the same standing
Rule 11b note as the last two rounds.

# 2026-07-27 (fourth attempt): character sums / Ramanujan sums (Li–Wan)

Authorized by the researcher to continue (round 4), who stated explicit trust in this session's
judgment over seeking an outside second opinion right now. **Restating the same standing note as
the last three rounds, unchanged by that**: this is the researcher's call to make about how to
spend effort, but it is not the independent mathematical verification Rule 11b calls for. Nothing
below should be trusted as a settled result on the strength of "the researcher said go ahead" or
"three prior rounds were self-consistent." This round's finding is more nuanced than the previous
three flat negatives, which makes the need for outside verification *higher*, not lower, before
any further weight is put on it.

## Verdict: the general framework is valid (verified numerically, unlike the previous two
attempts), but Li–Wan's specific closed-form machinery does not transfer, and the natural way to
use the framework anyway turns out to cost the same as the algorithm this project already runs.
No shortcut found. A concrete, hard open sub-problem is identified at the exact point where a
shortcut would need to come from.

### What Li–Wan's paper actually proves (read directly, `https://www.math.uci.edu/~dwan/liwan4.pdf`, fetched, converted with `pdftotext -layout`, and read section 1 in full)

J. Li and D. Wan, "Counting Subset Sums of Finite Abelian Groups." For $D\subseteq A$ a subset of
size $n$ of a finite abelian group $A$, $N_D(k,b)$ counts $k$-element subsets $S\subseteq D$ with
$\sum_{a\in S}a=b$ (a **plain, unweighted, order-independent** group sum). Their Theorem 1.1 gives
an exact formula for $N(k,b)$ when $D=A$ (the whole group), via
$$N(k,b)=\frac1n\sum_{r\mid(n,k)}(-1)^{k+k/r}\binom{n/r}{k/r}\Phi(r,b),\qquad
\Phi(r,b)=\sum_{d\mid r,\,(n_i,d)\mid b_i}\mu(r/d)\prod_i(n_i,d),$$
specializing for cyclic $A=\mathbb Z_n$ (Corollary 1.2) to a formula in terms of Ramanujan's sum
$C_r(b)=\sum_{d\mid(r,b)}\mu(r/d)\,d$. The derivation (Section 2, skimmed for method, not
reproduced in full here) uses the standard fact that a group character turns a sum into a
product, $\chi(a_1+\cdots+a_k)=\chi(a_1)\cdots\chi(a_k)$, then exploits that **every element of
the full group $A$ plays a symmetric role**, so the resulting character sum $\sum_{a\in A}\chi(a)$
collapses to a simple closed form (this is exactly Ramanujan's sum, whose Möbius-function formula
is classical) independent of which specific elements were "chosen."

### Setting up the analogous decomposition for $R_{l-1,j}$: valid, verified numerically

$N(c)$, the number of admissible tuples $(a_0>\cdots>a_{l-1})$ (from $\{0,\ldots,l+j-1\}$) with
$\sum_i 2^{a_i}3^i\equiv c\pmod{3^l}$, admits the same Fourier/character decomposition as any
function into $\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$ ($n=3^l$), by definition of the discrete Fourier transform,
regardless of any structure in how the tuples are chosen:
$$N(c)=\frac1n\sum_{t=0}^{n-1}e^{-2\pi i tc/n}\,S(t),\qquad
S(t)=\!\!\sum_{a_0>\cdots>a_{l-1}}\!\! e^{2\pi i t\left(\sum_i 2^{a_i}3^i\right)/n}.$$
Verified this numerically against the known checkpoint (l=2, j=2, direct enumeration gives
$N(0..8)=[0,1,2,0,0,2,0,1,0]$, matching {1,2,5,7} each once except 2 and 5 twice, missing 4 zeros
elsewhere, consistent with the known image $\{1,2,5,7\}$): computed $S(t)$ directly for all
$t=0,\ldots,8$ and inverted; the Fourier-recovered $N(c)$ matched the direct count exactly
(`[-0.0, 1.0, 2.0, -0.0, 0.0, 2.0, 0.0, 1.0, -0.0]` vs. direct `[0,1,2,0,0,2,0,1,0]`, differing
only by floating-point rounding of exact zeros). **This is a genuinely different outcome from the
first two attempts**: those failed to even produce a valid symmetric object for their respective
machinery to act on; this decomposition is unconditionally valid (it's just Fourier inversion),
and the numerical check confirms the $S(t)$ definition used is the correct one for this problem,
not a mis-transcription.

### Where it stops working: Li–Wan's closed form for $S(t)$-type sums needs element-genericity that $R_{j,k}$'s terms don't have

Character-turns-sum-into-product still holds here too:
$\chi(\sum_i 2^{a_i}3^i)=\prod_i\chi(2^{a_i}3^i)$, since this is a group-theoretic fact about any
additive decomposition, not specific to unweighted subset sums. The obstruction is not at this
step. It is in the *next* step, where Li–Wan use the fact that $D=A$ (or a generic subset) lets
them evaluate $\sum_{\text{choices}}\prod_i\chi(a_i)$ combinatorially, symmetric-function style,
because every element of the group is interchangeable with every other as far as the counting
argument is concerned. Here, the "elements" being summed are $2^{a_i}3^i$ for $a_i$ ranging over a
*shrinking, order-coupled* domain (each $a_i$ must be strictly less than the previous one, and the
weight $3^i$ is tied to *which pick number* $i$ this is, not to $a_i$ itself): the same
rank-dependence that blocked both prior attempts, restated here in the character-sum setting: there
is no known reduction of $S(t)$ to a Möbius/Ramanujan-sum closed form for this kind of
position-coupled, geometrically-structured ($2^{a_i}$, exponential in $a_i$) element family. Li–Wan's
paper, read in full for Section 1, contains no treatment of order-dependent or per-position-weighted
sums; every construction there is over an literal, unstructured subset of group elements.

### Trying to use the valid decomposition anyway: no shortcut, same cost as the existing algorithm

Since $S(t)$ has no known closed form here, the natural fallback is computing it directly. $S(t)$
admits an exact recursion by processing candidate values $a=l+j-1,\ldots,0$ from high to low and
tracking how many have been "taken" so far (the next taken value gets assigned the current count
as its rank $i$, exactly matching $R_{j,k}$'s definition): this is *structurally the same
transfer-process* as the bitset+rotation DP `experiments/E-001-jstar-fast/src/main.rs` already
implements, just tracking a single complex accumulator per residue class weighted by $t$ instead
of one bit per residue tracked simultaneously for all $t$ at once. Computing every $S(t)$ for
$t=0,\ldots,n-1$ this way costs at least as much as the existing DP (which computes the full
image in one pass, all residues simultaneously, via bitwise operations); there is no
computational or theoretical shortcut available through this route without an actual closed-form
evaluation of $S(t)$, which was not found.

### One structural fact surfaced, flagged but not pursued further

Checked (numerically, for $l=1..7$, and this generalizes by the standard lifting-the-exponent
argument since $\mathrm{ord}_3(2)=2$ and $2\not\equiv1\pmod9$): $\mathrm{ord}_{3^l}(2)=2\cdot3^{l-1}=\varphi(3^l)$
for every $l$ tested, i.e. **2 is a primitive root modulo $3^l$** for all $l$. This means
$\{2^a\bmod 3^l\}$ ranges over the *entire* unit group $(\mathbb Z/3^l\mathbb Z)^*$ as $a$ varies,
with a known, structured period. Whether this specific fact (2 being a primitive root, not just
some element) can be leveraged to actually evaluate or bound $S(t)$, turning it into a genuine
Gauss-sum-like object over the cyclic group generated by 2, is a real, concrete, more specific
question than "try character sums in general," and was not pursued further here for lack of time,
not because it looked unpromising. Flagging it as the most specific, actionable next sub-question
if this direction is pursued further, rather than "look for character sum methods" in general
(already done this round).

## Confidence

High confidence in the numerically-verified parts (the Fourier decomposition's validity, the
recursive structure of $S(t)$ being equivalent in cost to the existing DP, the primitive-root
fact). Medium-low confidence that character sums are a dead end *in principle*, unlike the first
two attempts, where a structural incompatibility was identified and confirmed, here the honest
state is "the specific paper found doesn't give a shortcut, and the obvious way to use the
framework costs the same as what we already do," which is a weaker, more contingent kind of
negative result: it does not rule out that a cleverer closed-form evaluation of $S(t)$ exists
(exploiting the primitive-root structure, or known bounds on exponential sums of geometric
sequences mod prime powers, a classical but genuinely hard area of analytic number theory), only
that this session did not find one in the time spent.

**What needs a second opinion, explicitly, before further investment**: whether the primitive-root
structure of 2 mod $3^l$ makes $S(t)$ tractable via known exponential-sum (Weyl-sum-style) bounds
from analytic number theory is a genuine open question this round did not resolve either way. This
is exactly the kind of judgment call: is this worth a serious time investment, or a plausible-looking
dead end, that Rule 11b says needs an independent view, not this session's own momentum across
four consecutive self-authorized rounds.

## Recommendation

H-003 stays `open-unexplored`. Four approaches now attempted: Bajnok's survey (ruled out,
structural mismatch), direct polynomial method/Erdős–Heilbronn (ruled out, ring + structural
mismatch), the ordered-domain reframing (ruled out, category error, no symmetry to exploit), and
character sums/Ramanujan sums (this round: framework valid but no closed form found, no shortcut
over the existing algorithm, one concrete unexplored sub-question flagged: exploiting 2's
primitive-root structure for a Weyl-sum-style bound on $S(t)$). Before a fifth round, this project
should get an actual outside opinion (different-vendor model or the researcher's own background),
not another self-authorized continuation: four rounds of this session's own increasingly intricate
self-consistent reasoning is exactly the situation Rule 11b exists to catch, not a substitute for
it.

# 2026-07-28 (fifth attempt): genuine outside consultation (Gemini) on the coupon-collector /
$p_{\min}(l)$ lead, plus fresh $L^2$/argmin diagnostics

The researcher obtained the outside opinion the fourth round asked for: consulted Gemini
(Google, a genuinely different vendor, not another same-model round) with a full write-up of all
four prior attempts and the verified data. This round continues that consultation in an active
back-and-forth loop (`python3 consult_gemini.py`, a small script wrapping the Gemini API,
`gemini-3.6-flash`, the strongest model this API key has quota for; Pro-tier models returned
quota errors, confirmed, not pursued further), verifying every claim it made rather than trusting
it, per this project's Rule 8c ("verify a critique before acting on it") applied to a
consultation, not just a critique.

## What Gemini's first two answers established (already done by the researcher before this round)

Gemini's first suggestion (L2-flattening / Fourier decay, analogous to Tao's Rajchman-measure
approach to beta=1) was tested empirically: computed $\sum_{t\ne0}|S(t)|^2/M^2$ at $j=j^*(l)$
exactly, for $l=1..11$ (FFT on the exact value-histogram of $R_{l-1,j}\bmod3^l$, built from
`itertools.combinations` enumeration, verified against the known $l=2,j=2$ checkpoint). Result:
$0.50,0.72,0.71,0.82,0.83,0.91,1.00,1.08,1.16,1.11,1.18$, growing, not decaying toward
$3^{-l}$. Gemini correctly re-derived (Cauchy-Schwarz/Parseval, checked and confirmed correct
independently below) that this means $L^2$ methods hit a "second-moment barrier" familiar from
random-graph/hypergraph threshold phenomena: at $j^*(l)$, $L^2$ machinery can only ever certify
$\approx46\%$ coverage, not the full $100\%$ that has actually already happened (verified: with
$\text{L2\_excl0}=1.18$, $|\text{supp}|\ge n/(1+1.18)\approx0.459n$, matches Gemini's number).

A follow-up computed min/max hit-count per invertible residue, exactly at $j=j^*(l)$, $l=1..11$:
min is $1$ (occasionally $2,4,5$) in every case, max grows into the hundreds ($l=11$: min$=4$,
max$=401$). This is a real, verified empirical fact: $j^*(l)$ is governed by the least-visited
residue (a "coupon collector" / extreme-value phenomenon), not by average/mean-field behavior.
Gemini's resulting concrete proposal: estimate $p_{\min}(l)$ (the minimum per-tuple hit
probability over residues), model $E[\#\text{unvisited}]\approx 2\cdot3^{l-1}\cdot
e^{-M(j)p_{\min}(l)}$, solve for the threshold. It cited three references: Flajolet, Gardy &
Thimonier 1992 (non-uniform coupon collector); the "Matthews bound" for Markov-chain cover times
(Aldous & Fill, Ch. 6); and an unnamed "Fernandez & Galatolo 2020" shrinking-target-problem paper.

## Citation verification (Rule 11: a consultation's answer is not itself a primary source)

Asked Gemini to self-report exact bibliographic detail and its own confidence per citation, then
independently verified via web search and, for the first, primary-source text extraction:

- **Flajolet, Gardy & Thimonier (1992), Discrete Applied Mathematics 39(3):207-229**: real,
  confirmed via web search (ScienceDirect, INRIA's own copy at
  `https://algo.inria.fr/flajolet/Publications/FlGaTh92.pdf`, `10.1016/0166-218X(92)90177-C`).
  Fetched the PDF and ran `pdftotext` on it (an old scanned paper, imperfect OCR, but legible)
  and located **Theorem 4.1 / formula (13b)** directly: for a full collection under a general
  (non-uniform) probability distribution $p_1,\ldots,p_m$,
  $$E(C_m)=\int_0^\infty\Big(1-\prod_{i=1}^m(1-e^{-p_it})\Big)\,dt,$$
  matching Gemini's quoted formula essentially verbatim (OCR noise aside). Corollary 4.2's
  inclusion-exclusion sum form is also present, matching Gemini's second formula. **This
  citation and its exact formula are confirmed against the primary source, not just Gemini's
  say-so.**
- **Matthews (1988), "Covering Problems for Markov Chains," Annals of Probability 16(3),
  pp.1215-1228** (Gemini said 1216, off by one page in its self-report, negligible): real, exists
  (confirmed via web search, Project Euclid listing matches author/year/journal/volume/topic).
  Did **not** fetch and verify the exact inequality Gemini quoted (Matthews' bound formula
  itself) against the primary text in this round; that specific formula is **unverified**, only
  the paper's existence and general topic are confirmed. Not used further below since the
  Flajolet-Gardy-Thimonier route turned out to be the relevant one and Matthews' bound wasn't
  actually needed for what follows.
- **"M. Fernandez, S. Galatolo 2020"**: asked Gemini to self-assess; it retracted this one
  itself, unprompted, rating its own confidence 0% and stating plainly "this citation appears to
  be a hallucinated reference... there is no published paper or arXiv preprint matching this
  author pair/year/topic combination." Treated as non-existent, not used.

## Methodological pushback: is the coupon-collector heuristic even valid here?

Before building on Gemini's $p_{\min}(l)$ proposal, pushed back on the core assumption: $R_{l-1,j}$
is a **deterministic, exhaustive enumeration** of all $\binom{l+j}{l}$ tuples, not $M(j)$
independent random draws. Asked directly whether treating it as an i.i.d. coupon-collector
process is rigorously justified (e.g. via a known Poissonization technique) or an unjustified
importation of an independence assumption.

**Gemini's answer, direct and self-critical**: no, there is no such justification. Poissonization
converts a fixed *random* sample size into a Poisson-distributed one; it cannot convert a rigid,
deterministic combinatorial enumeration into i.i.d. random variables. To rigorously justify
treating the deterministic sequence as pseudorandom enough for the coupon-collector heuristic to
give the *correct* threshold (not just a plausible-looking heuristic guess at the right order of
magnitude), one would need to bound exactly the same exponential sum $S(\xi)$ that the fourth
round already showed has no known closed form. Gemini's own words: "bridging the gap between
'this deterministic object behaves pseudorandomly' and 'this deterministic object is truly
random' is almost always 90% of the actual work," and explicitly: "Do not use it in a formal
proof... only as a predictive sanity check."

**This is a real, useful negative finding in itself**: the coupon-collector framing that
motivated this entire round is not, on its own, a path to a rigorous bound. It could still be
useful as a heuristic for guessing the right asymptotic *shape* of $j^*(l)$ (e.g. is $e(l)$
closer to $\log l$, $\sqrt l$, or linear, the exact open question E-002 is already trying to
resolve statistically), but not as a route to an actual proof, contrary to how it was originally
framed by Gemini's first answer in this project's live session.

## Two more sub-directions offered, both checked and both dead ends

Gemini offered two ways to make the heuristic rigorous instead of abandoning it: (A) Fourier /
second-moment bounding of $S(\xi)$ directly (bound $\sum_{\xi\ne0}|S(\xi)|$ small enough to force
$N(r)>0$ for every $r$), and (B) Erdős–Turán discrepancy theory.

**Pushed back again**: pointed out that (A) is not new, it is exactly the character-sum route
the fourth round already attempted and found no closed form for, and asked whether (B) offers any
technique the fourth round didn't already try. **Gemini's answer, direct**: no. "Erdős-Turán does
not solve the bottleneck; it relies entirely on the solver having already conquered $S(\xi)$...
Erdős-Turán actually loses precision compared to direct $L^2$ Fourier analysis." Confirmed: both
(A) and (B) are the same already-blocked obstruction restated in different classical vocabulary,
not new ground.

## A third sub-direction (hypergraph/3-adic covering) offered as genuinely different: caught as fabricated, retracted by Gemini itself

Gemini then offered a third framing ("Framing C"): a hypergraph-covering argument using
Lifting-the-Exponent (LTE) 3-adic valuations on "step differences" between adjacent tuples,
claimed to avoid exponential sums entirely. Asked for a fully worked, checkable example against
$l=5$ ($j^*(5)=9$) and $l=6$ ($j^*(6)=10$).

**The worked example did not survive scrutiny.** Gemini computed the number of tuples as
$\binom{j}{l}$ (e.g. $\binom{9}{5}=126$ for $l=5,j=9$), but the correct ground-set size per this
project's own definition is $l+j$ (not $j$), giving $\binom{l+j}{l}=\binom{14}{5}=2002$, a value
this project had **already computed and verified by direct enumeration earlier in this same
round** ($M=2002$ confirmed for $l=5,j=9$ in the min/max diagnostic above). Pointed this out
explicitly and asked Gemini to redo the calculation with the correct $M$ and to honestly assess
whether the original argument still held.

**Gemini's own post-mortem, quoted directly because it is itself a useful finding**: "the entire
arithmetic framework of 'Framing C' falls apart completely... No, Framing C is not a real,
first-principles mathematical derivation. It was a post-hoc rationalization (a mathematical
hallucination) engineered to fit the known empirical values $j^*(5)=9$ and $j^*(6)=10$... By
mistakenly using $\binom{j}{l}$ instead of $\binom{l+j}{l}$, the count... came out to
$\binom{9}{5}=126$. Because $\log_3(126)\approx4.39$, it was trivial to invent a pseudo-
theoretical rule ('capacity bound $\approx l-1=4$') that appeared to fit $j=9$ perfectly."

This is recorded in full because it is a concrete, caught instance of exactly the failure mode
this project's honesty rules exist to catch (Rule 11: never trust a plausible-sounding derivation
without verifying it against ground truth), happening in the external consultation this project
sought specifically as a safeguard against this session's own errors. The lesson is symmetric:
an outside opinion is a genuine, valuable check, but it is not automatically more trustworthy
than this session's own reasoning, and needs the same verification discipline applied to it.

## Independent follow-up: does the "coldest" residue have a clean closed form?

Not suggested by Gemini; attempted independently, since the parent instructions asked for an
analytic (not just numeric) handle on $p_{\min}(l)$. Computed, for $l=1..11$ at $j=j^*(l)$, which
specific residue(s) achieve the minimum hit-count, and checked against several structurally
plausible candidates (the multiplicative inverse of 2 mod $3^l$, $n-1$, $2\cdot3^{l-1}-1$): none
of these match the actual argmin residues found by exact enumeration, and the argmin locations
(e.g. $l=9$: five different residues tied at min$=1$, scattered: $1048, 7051, 13927, 17059,
17815$ out of $19683$) show no obvious simple pattern. This is a negative finding, not a
non-finding: it's mild further evidence (not proof) that $p_{\min}(l)$ likely does not have an
easy closed form reachable by inspection, consistent with the fourth round's finding that $S(t)$
has none either.

## Confidence

High confidence in everything verified against a primary source or independent computation
(both citation checks with primary-source confirmation for Flajolet-Gardy-Thimonier; the
Cauchy-Schwarz re-derivation; the Framing-C arithmetic error and its retraction; the argmin
computation). Medium confidence, clearly flagged as such by Gemini itself and not contradicted
by anything found here, that the coupon-collector heuristic's *qualitative* framing (extreme-value
governed, not mean-field) is correct even though a *rigorous* path from it to a bound was not
found this round.

**What still needs a second opinion, explicitly**: whether there is a rigorous way to bound
$p_{\min}(l)$ (or the argmin residue's hit-count) directly, by some route that does not pass
through bounding $S(\xi)$ (which four consultations/rounds have now converged on being the
central, unresolved bottleneck under multiple names: character sums, Fourier coefficients,
Erdős-Turán discrepancy terms). This is the same open question the fourth round already flagged
(2 being a primitive root mod $3^l$, possibly enabling a genuine Weyl-sum-style bound), now
reinforced from a second, independent angle rather than resolved.

## Recommendation

H-003 stays `open-unexplored`. Five rounds now attempted, four self-authorized within one AI
session and a fifth genuinely cross-checked against an independent model (Gemini), which is
itself the outside opinion the fourth round called for. All five converge on the same underlying
obstruction: bounding the exponential sum $S(\xi)=\sum_{a_0>\cdots>a_{l-1}}
e(2\pi i\,\xi\sum_k2^{a_k}3^k/3^l)$ for $\xi\ne0$, restated in five different vocabularies
(sumset critical numbers, Combinatorial Nullstellensatz, ordered-domain symmetry, Ramanujan
sums, coupon-collector/discrepancy), none of which supply a known closed form or nontrivial bound
for this specific position-coupled, mixed-base structure. The one concrete, unresolved,
repeatedly-flagged sub-question across rounds 4 and 5 is whether 2 being a primitive root mod
$3^l$ enables an actual Weyl-sum-style bound on $S(\xi)$; this is the most specific remaining
lead, not a new broad direction. Given five rounds' convergent difficulty on the same single
obstruction, the productive next step (if pursued at all) is a targeted attempt at bounding
$S(\xi)$ specifically using the primitive-root structure, not another round surveying new
top-level frameworks; that survey now looks exhausted.

# 2026-07-28 (sixth attempt): 3-adic transfer operators / thermodynamic formalism

Continuing the autonomous overnight loop with Gemini (the researcher is asleep, authorized to
keep going). Round 5 flagged that five framings had converged on the same obstruction (bounding
$S(\xi)$). This round tries the one remaining top-level direction Gemini's very first answer
called out as genuinely different in kind: 3-adic transfer operators / thermodynamic formalism,
rather than another counting/character-sum reformulation.

## Setting up the operator concretely, not accepting a sketch

Gave Gemini the exact Wirsching back-tracing correspondence established in `notes/H-004.md`
tonight (feasible vectors $s=(s_0,\ldots,s_k)$, $v_s=T_0^{-s_0}\circ T_1^{-1}\circ\cdots$,
$T_0^{-1}(x)=2x$, $T_1^{-1}(x)=(2x-1)/3$) and asked for a fully explicit operator: function
space, kernel, and the precise translation of "$R_{j,k}$ covers mod $3^l$" into a spectral
statement, with an explicit instruction to say immediately if it also reduces to $S(\xi)$ rather
than build out a whole framework first.

**The operator it gave**: on $\mathcal V_l=\{f:(\mathbb Z/3^l\mathbb Z)^*\to\mathbb C\}$, with a
fugacity $z$ tracking doubling-weight,
$$(\mathcal L_z f)(y)=\sum_{b\ge0}z^b\,f(3y+2^{1+b}\bmod3^l),$$
and covering translates to strict positivity of $[z^{\le j}](\mathcal L_z^l\mathbf 1)(x)$ at
every invertible residue $x$.

## Gemini's own answer to "does this reduce to $S(\xi)$ too": yes, and it proved it, not just asserted it

Diagonalizing $\mathcal L_z$ in the additive-character basis $\chi_\xi(x)=e(2\pi i\xi x/3^l)$:
$$(\mathcal L_z\chi_\xi)(y)=\chi_{3\xi}(y)\cdot\Big(\sum_{b\ge0}z^b\,e(2\pi i\,\xi\,2^{1+b}/3^l)\Big),$$
i.e. $\chi_\xi$ is (up to a scalar) mapped to $\chi_{3\xi}$: characters are eigenvectors of the
"multiply-the-argument-by-3" shift this operator performs, a standard fact once you notice
$\mathcal L_z$'s action is built from a group translation composed with a fixed shift. Iterating
$l$ times sends $\xi\mapsto3^l\xi\equiv0\pmod{3^l}$, collapsing back to the trivial character, and
the accumulated scalar factor is exactly the same exponential sum $S(\xi)$ every prior round has
been unable to bound, restated as the operator's spectrum under Fourier diagonalization.

## Independently re-verified, not taken on Gemini's word

Two checks, both pass:
- **The core factorization step** ($\chi_\xi(3y+2^{1+b})=\chi_{3\xi}(y)\cdot\chi_\xi(2^{1+b})$),
  verified numerically for a concrete case ($n=9,\xi=2,y=5,b=1$): exact match to machine precision.
- **Consistency with $R_{j,k}$'s actual definition**: worked through by hand whether the
  operator's free per-step choice of $b_k\ge0$ (no explicit ordering constraint visible in the
  operator's definition) is really the same object as $R_{j,k}$'s explicit strictly-decreasing
  exponent sequence $a_0>a_1>\cdots$. It is: this is exactly the gap-reparametrization
  ($b_i$ = the doubling-count *between* consecutive picks) that `notes/H-004.md` already
  identified as identical to Tao's Syrac construction (`literature/notes/L-003.md`'s $g_i$
  reindexing). The ordering constraint isn't dropped by the operator formulation, it's encoded
  differently (sequentially, one $b_k$ per iteration, position $k$ fixed by which power of
  $\mathcal L_z$ you're inside) rather than via an explicit sort-then-rank step. Confirmed the
  homomorphism property $\chi_\xi(\sum_i2^{a_i}3^i)=\prod_i\chi_\xi(2^{a_i}3^i)$ holds exactly
  for 5 sample tuples from the verified $l=2,j=2$ case.

## Why this is a more valuable negative result than rounds 1-5, not just a sixth data point

This isn't "another framework happens to also hit the wall" (rounds 1-5's pattern). It's a
**proof of why any framework built from the additive structure of $\mathbb Z/3^l\mathbb Z$ must
hit this exact wall**: characters are the canonical (in fact, essentially unique up to
change-of-basis) diagonalizing basis for any operator or counting problem that respects a finite
abelian group's translation structure. Counting (rounds 1, 4), symmetry-breaking (round 2, 3),
coupon-collector/discrepancy (round 5), and now spectral/dynamical (round 6) are not six
independent attempts that coincidentally all failed the same way; they are six different
*vocabularies* for the same underlying Fourier-analytic question, and Fourier analysis on a
finite abelian group is, structurally, unavoidable machinery for any of them. Asked Gemini
directly whether it agrees this is now a meta-level conclusion rather than "try a seventh
framework"; it agreed, and could not name a genuinely non-character-theoretic angle when asked
directly to try.

**Correction, 2026-07-30 (full critique round): this meta-conclusion is false as stated, refuted
by this project's own later work.** Rounds 19-21 (see below and `experiments/E-003-mpg-cylinder/`)
built exactly the kind of framework this section claims cannot exist: a genuine, working framework
on the additive/multiplicative structure of `(Z/3^k Z)^*` (a controlled-3-adic-dynamics mean-payoff
game) that does NOT reduce to `S(t)` and uses no Fourier analysis anywhere (H-009's own row states
this explicitly), and it produced every good bound this project has. The claim "any framework built
from the additive structure of `Z/3^l Z` must hit this exact wall" is empirically refuted by the
project's own most successful result. A specific technical point behind the overreach (raised by
the critique, not independently re-derived here, flagged as attributed rather than settled):
characters diagonalize TRANSLATION-INVARIANT (convolution) operators specifically; the operator
built in this round sends `chi_xi` to `chi_{3xi}`, which is a shift on the character index, not
obviously a diagonalization in the usual sense, and `xi -> 3xi` is not even a bijection on
`Z/3^l Z` (3 is a zero divisor there, not a unit), which undercuts the "essentially unique up to
change of basis" step. This paragraph's meta-claim should not be relied on or repeated; the
narrower, actually-verified fact from this round is that THIS SPECIFIC operator's construction
reduces to `S(t)`, not that every possible framework must.

## Confidence

High. The operator's definition and its character-diagonalization were checked step by step
(not accepted from Gemini's derivation alone), including a numerical spot-check of the key
factorization identity and a hand-verification that the operator's implicit ordering matches
$R_{j,k}$'s explicit one via the already-established gap-reparametrization from H-004. This is
not another instance of round 5's caught fabrication (Framing C): the algebra here is standard,
transparent, and independently reproduced, not a plausible-looking construction that collapses
under a numerical check.

## Recommendation

**Six rounds now converge on the same obstruction, the sixth with an actual derivation of why
any Fourier-respecting approach must.** Per the parent instruction: this is the point to stop
self-directed rounds and wait for the researcher, not launch a seventh. The concrete open
sub-question flagged since round 4 stands as the one genuinely specific remaining lead: whether
2's primitive-root structure mod $3^l$ enables a real Weyl-sum-style bound on $S(\xi)$, a
targeted analytic-number-theory attack on one specific exponential sum, not another top-level
framework search, and even that needs to grapple with the ordering/position-coupling that makes
$S(\xi)$ resist every classical technique tried so far. H-003 stays `open-unexplored`; nothing
here should be read as ruling out that some approach exists, only that six well-executed,
cross-checked attempts (five internal, one genuinely external and adversarially verified) have
not found it, and the meta-level reason they keep converging is now understood, which is itself
worth having established.

# 2026-07-28 (literature search, not a seventh framework round): targeted search for a bound on S(ξ)

Per round 6's explicit recommendation (pause self-directed framework rounds, the two remaining
legitimate paths are new literature or the researcher's own input), this is a scoped literature
search, not a math-derivation attempt. Searched for existing analytic-number-theory results that
could bound $S(\xi)$ directly, focused on the one concrete open sub-question repeated since round
4: does 2 being a primitive root mod $3^l$ enable a Weyl-sum-style bound?

## What was found: a real, structurally close match, with the same limitation as every prior round

**T. Cochrane's exponential-sums-modulo-prime-powers research program** (L-019, L-020 in
`literature/INDEX.md`) develops exactly the relevant classical machinery: for a fixed primitive
root $a$ mod $p^m$, the $p$-adic logarithm converts the multiplicative action of a character on
$a^k$ into a **linear function of $k$** (L-019 eq. 1.4-1.5: $\chi(a^k) = e\big(2\pi i\,ck /
(p^{m-1}(p-1))\big)$). Substituting $p=3$, $a=2$ (our primitive root, verified in round 4) lines
up structurally with $H$-003's setting about as closely as anything found across six framework
rounds and this search.

**The limitation, found immediately and not glossed over**: every sum Cochrane's method treats is
a **complete sum over the full residue range** $x=1,\ldots,p^m$ (or a full coset structure derived
from it). $S(\xi) = \sum_{a_0>a_1>\cdots>a_{l-1}} e(2\pi i\,\xi\sum_k 2^{a_k}3^k/3^l)$ sums over a
strictly-decreasing-sequence-restricted domain, a small, rank-coupled proper subset of all
possible exponent tuples, not a complete range. This is the exact same restricted-domain
obstruction that blocked Bajnok (round 1), the polynomial method (rounds 2-3), and Li-Wan (round
4). Cochrane's linearization trick for the primitive-root power is real and directly relevant, but
it addresses a different part of the problem than the part that has actually been blocking every
attempt; it does not by itself resolve the restricted-domain issue.

## Other searches, no relevant hit

- "Rank-weighted subset sum," "position-weighted exponential sum," "layered sumset," "ordered
  subset sum character sum," "sum over decreasing sequences exponential sum": no hits beyond what
  round 1/2's searches already found (nothing).
- Papers citing Wirsching 1998 outside the already-catalogued Collatz-specific literature
  (`literature/INDEX.md` L-001 through L-020): none found; Wirsching's book appears to be cited
  almost exclusively within the Collatz/3x+1 literature, not picked up by the general analytic
  exponential-sums community.
- Index-calculus / discrete-log exponential sum literature (cryptography angle): surfaced only
  standard discrete-log algorithm complexity results (index calculus, Pohlig-Hellman), nothing
  about bounding structured sums of primitive-root powers over a restricted index domain.

## Recommendation

Not close to a resolved lead, but a real, well-sourced, specific candidate for an eventual round
7 (or the researcher's own attempt): **adapt Cochrane's p-adic-logarithm linearization of
$2^{a_i}$ (from L-019/L-020) to $S(\xi)$'s specific rank-coupled domain**, i.e. try to combine
Cochrane's primitive-root-to-linear conversion with whatever handles the ordering constraint
(nothing found in this search or the prior six rounds handles that part). This is not a synthesis
attempted here (per the parent task's explicit instruction not to restart the derivation process
this round); it is a concrete, checkable starting point for whoever picks this up next. L-019
Sections 2+ (the full critical-point machinery) and the two Acta Arithmetica papers L-020
references but doesn't itself contain are the next things to actually read if this is pursued.

# 2026-07-28 (broadened literature search, whole internet, not arXiv-restricted)

Per the researcher's explicit request to search beyond arXiv. Still a literature-search task, not
a math-derivation round, per round 6's recommendation. Searched general web (not just arXiv) for:
MathOverflow/Stack Exchange discussions, textbooks/lecture notes, coding theory (rank modulation
codes, checked properly this time rather than inherited-dismissed), combinatorics-on-words /
lattice-path framings, Wirsching's broader bibliography beyond L-001/L-002, and mixed-radix
base-(2,3) representation literature directly.

## Closest new hit: Anisimov's mixed-base-(2,3) representation research program

**A. V. Anisimov, "Integer representation in the mixed base (2,3)," Cybernetics and Systems
Analysis 45 (2009), 503-516**, plus follow-ups "Prefix Encoding by Means of the (2,3)-
Representation of Numbers" (IEEE Trans. Info. Theory 59:4, 2013, 2359-2374) and "Generating
(2,3)-Codes" (2020). This is, by title and abstract, a mixed-base-2-and-3 numeration system,
structurally the closest-sounding match found in this entire investigation (six framework rounds
plus two literature passes) to R_{j,k}'s own mixed-base 2^{a_i}3^i construction.

**Not verified beyond abstract-level, and flagged as such rather than overclaimed**: every copy
found is paywalled (Springer, IEEE); no free preprint located on arXiv, ResearchGate (request-only,
not directly downloadable), or elsewhere in this search. From the abstracts alone, Anisimov's stated
motivation is **prefix-free integer encoding for error-tolerant transmission**, not covering or
surjectivity of a fixed-length family onto residues mod 3^l, a different specific question, even
though the underlying number system may be the same or closely related object. Whether it contains
anything transferable to $S(\xi)$ cannot be determined without reading the actual paper, which
requires institutional access this session doesn't have. Logged as a real, specific, findable lead
for the researcher to pursue via their own institutional access, not as a resolved or dismissed
result either way.

## Checked properly, not just inherited: rank modulation / permutation codes

Read past the abstract level this time (per the parent task's instruction to overturn or confirm
round 4's dismissal with an actual look). Confirmed: rank modulation codes (flash-memory error
correction, Jiang/Schwartz/Bruck and related work) concern the **Kendall-tau metric on
permutations**: codes are packings of the symmetric group under a rearrangement distance, entirely
about correcting *which permutation* was intended, not about summing values weighted by their
ordinal position in a chosen subset. Round 4's dismissal holds up under a real check, not just an
inherited assumption: this field's notion of "rank" is unrelated to $R_{j,k}$'s rank-dependent
summation weight.

## Other searches, genuinely tried, no hit

- **MathOverflow/Stack Exchange**: no discussion found addressing exponential sums with
  rank/position-coupled index domains modulo a prime power specifically; general search engines do
  not appear to index a directly relevant thread, if one exists.
- **"Variants of Base 3 over 2" (arXiv:1901.09818)**: fetched and read directly (not just the
  search snippet): a fractional-base ("3/2") numeration system, unrelated to R_{j,k}'s integer
  mixed-base-2,3 structure and unrelated to covering/exponential sums. False lead from title
  similarity alone; ruled out by actually reading it, not by title.
- **Wirsching's broader bibliography**: found two papers not yet in `literature/INDEX.md`: "Über
  das 3n+1 Problem" (survey, Elem. Math. 55, 2000, 142-155) and "A functional differential equation
  and 3n+1 dynamics" (Fields Inst. Commun. 29, 2001, 369-378). Neither has a freely accessible copy
  found in this search; both logged in the index as found-but-unread rather than skipped silently.
  Given the survey (2000) predates and the FDE paper (2001) is methodologically distant from
  (differential-equations, not combinatorial/exponential-sum) this project's specific question,
  neither looks like a strong candidate on title/venue alone, but this is not a substitute for
  actually reading them.
- **Combinatorics-on-words / lattice-path-with-modular-target framing**: searched directly, no
  literature found treating "count lattice paths / subset choices by a positional-weight sum target
  mod $p^k$" as a named problem type.

## Recommendation

A genuinely broader search (beyond arXiv, across forums/textbooks/adjacent fields/Wirsching's full
bibliography) surfaced one real, specific, findable-but-unread lead (Anisimov's mixed-base-(2,3)
work, paywalled) and confirmed one prior dismissal properly rather than inheriting it uncritically
(rank modulation codes are a genuine non-fit, verified not assumed). No new technique was found
that bounds $S(\xi)$ directly. This strengthens, rather than overturns, round 6's assessment: the
specific combinatorial obstruction this project has isolated does not appear to have off-the-shelf
literature coverage under any vocabulary tried across eight search passes (six framework rounds'
embedded searches plus two dedicated literature passes). H-003 stays `open-unexplored`. The
concrete next actions, in priority order: (1) the researcher obtaining Anisimov's papers via
institutional access and checking them properly, since title/topic proximity alone isn't
resolution either way; (2) the Cochrane-linearization-plus-ordering-constraint synthesis flagged in
the prior literature-search section; (3) the researcher's own mathematical input, per round 6's

# 2026-07-28: context from H-004 (not a new round, no new attempt at S(t))

The researcher obtained Wirsching's full 1998 book overnight; `notes/H-004.md` was updated with a
precise, numerically-verified resolution of that hypothesis's own gap (see there for detail). One
piece of what was found is directly relevant context for H-003, recorded here rather than in
H-004 since it bears on why j*(l) is hard, not on the R_{j,k}/back-tracing identity itself.

Wirsching's own "(j,k,l)-locally/globally covering triple" framework (Chapter V, Definitions
1.1-1.13) requires the back-tracing vector length to be strictly less than the target covering
modulus exponent (his own notation: `j<l`). This project's j*(l) question, via the now-precise
R_{J-1,J}=R(F_{J,J}) identity, is exactly the degenerate `j=l` boundary case: vector length equal
to, not less than, the modulus exponent. Wirsching's own recursive machinery for building
coverage of a finer modulus out of coverage at a coarser one (his Lemma 1.3, an inductive estimate
relating `e_l` to `e_{l-j}`) is built for the `j<l` regime and does not obviously extend to the
`j=l` boundary this project's j*(l) sits at.

This is not a seventh framework attempt and no claim is made that it unblocks anything: it is
context that the *primary source itself*, read now in full for the relevant chapters, already
frames this project's exact question as a boundary case its own author's techniques don't reach,
independent of and consistent with all six rounds' shared S(t) obstruction. Whether Lemma 1.3's
estimate says anything nontrivial as `j -> l` (rather than strictly `j<l`) was not worked out here
and is flagged as a possible, unexplored angle for a future round, not pursued in this pass.

# 2026-07-28: Wirsching 2000 survey's "Satz 4.2" checked against H-004 - confirmatory, not new

The researcher obtained (institutional access) G.J. Wirsching's 2000 survey "Über das 3n+1
Problem" (Elem. Math. 55, 142-155; L-022, previously catalogued as "found but unread"). Read in
full. Section 4 states, in more elementary notation than the 1998 book, a result the parent
session's initial read flagged as *possibly* a new bijection-type theorem distinct from H-004's
already-established `R_{J-1,J} = R(F_{J,J})` identity. **Verdict after checking: it is the same
fact as H-004's Corollary 1.8, restated for a general audience, not a new result and not a
covering/surjectivity statement.**

## The object, precisely

For a Collatz-graph path `b -> T(b) -> ... -> a` with `k` steps of type T0 and `l` steps of type
T1, numbering the path's edges `0,...,k+l-1` and writing `alpha_0 > alpha_1 > ... > alpha_{l-1}`
for the edge-numbers of the T1-steps (in reverse order), the paper derives a "Vorgängerformel"
(predecessor formula) `b = (2^{k+l}*a - Rest)/3^l`, where
`Rest = sum_{j=0}^{l-1} 2^{alpha_j} * 3^j`. This `Rest` is exactly this project's `R_{j,k}` sum
(`j=l-1` terms, same strictly-decreasing-exponent structure), confirmed by the paper's own
definition two paragraphs later of `S_{k,l} := {Rest : k+l > alpha_0 > ... > alpha_{l-1} >= 0}`,
which is `R_{j,k}` verbatim under the relabeling `l=j+1`.

**"Satz 4.2" (attributed to the 1998 book, i.e. not new to this survey) says**: for fixed `l`, as
`a` ranges over the `2*3^{l-1}` residues coprime to 3 mod `3^l`, every UNBOUNDED
strictly-decreasing `l`-tuple `(alpha_0,...,alpha_{l-1}) in N_0^l` (no cap on `alpha_0`, unlike
`S_{k,l}`) occurs exactly once in a Vorgängerformel.

## Why this is not a covering/bijection theorem in the sense H-003 needs

Read literally, "exactly once" cannot mean a numeric bijection between the (countably infinite)
set of unbounded tuples and the (finite, size `2*3^{l-1}`) set of residues - a finite target can't
biject with an infinite source. The paper's own next paragraph (defining `S_{k,l}`) states
directly, citing the 1998 book: distinct `l`-tuples always give distinct *exact* (unreduced)
`Rest` values, not distinct values *mod* `3^l`. Verified this numerically before trusting the
reading: for `l=2`, all 28 tuples with `alpha_0 in 0..7` give 28 distinct exact `Rest` values (no
collisions); but reducing mod 9, the (bounded, `S_{2,2}=R_{1,2}`) tuples collide onto only 4
values (`{1,2,5,7}`, the project's own known checkpoint) - e.g. `(1,0)` and `(3,1)` both give 5
mod 9. So "occurs exactly once in a Vorgängerformel" is a statement about each tuple determining a
unique, well-defined *admissibility residue class* `a mod 3^l` (the class for which the
back-tracing path is arithmetically consistent throughout), not a statement that residues get
covered exactly once by tuples. This is precisely H-004's already-verified `R_{J-1,J}=R(F_{J,J})`
fact (an admissible vector maps to one residue) in the survey's less formal 2000 presentation, not
an independent or stronger result.

## Verdict

**Confirmatory of H-004, not a new finding, does not bear on j*(l) beyond what H-004 already
established.** Satz 4.2 answers "which single residue class is a given back-tracing tuple
consistent with," the same question H-004's Corollary 1.8 already answered precisely and
numerically verified. It says nothing about whether a *bounded-length* family like `R_{j,k}`
*surjects onto all* `2*3^{l-1}` residues as `j` grows, which remains exactly j*(l)'s open question
and exactly where all six H-003 rounds' shared `S(t)` obstruction still applies. No new bound,
asymptotic, or technique for `S(t)` found here. Medium-high confidence in this reading (grounded
in the paper's own explicit "distinct tuples, distinct exact sums" statement plus independent
numerical re-verification, not just a single parse of the German); as with every prior round, a
genuine hard-judgment read of a primary source's terse theorem statement should still get a second
opinion before being treated as fully settled, per Rule 11b.

## Recommendation

No change to H-003's status or to the standing recommendation (paused, pending new literature or
researcher input). `literature/INDEX.md`'s L-022 updated from "not found freely accessible" to
read-and-assessed.
