# 2026-07-29 (twentieth round): deepening the Bellman/cylinder direction -- Codex's own follow-up
# is untrustworthy this time (admitted tool failures), but an independent search of my own found no
# improvement below rho=2

Following the researcher's request to push the one thread that actually produced a result (round
19's `j*(l)<=2l`) rather than start a fresh broad brainstorm, sent gpt-5.6-sol a targeted follow-up
asking it to (a) search for a larger cylinder certificate (mod 81 or mod 243) beating `rho=2`, and
(b) estimate the critical average cost `rho* = inf_h sup_z min_d(d+h(T_d z)-h(z))` directly, with
real sandbox access to run its own code this time. Full transcript:
`/home/rat/.claude/jobs/a8d2d60e/tmp/codex_sol_deepen_response.txt`.

## Codex's response is not trustworthy this round, by its own admission

The model reported specific numbers -- a claimed pattern `rho_k = 1 + 2/(k-1)` for `k=3,4,5`, "three
exceptional values" at `k=6,7,8`, a claimed rigorous interval `log_4(3) <= rho* <= 6/5`, and a table
of "118,098 states" -- but its own final paragraph states plainly: *"every host command aborted
before execution with `bwrap: loopback: Failed RTM_NEWADDR`. Repository writes failed at the same
boundary, so I could not persist the CSV/search code in the workspace."* Confirmed independently:
no files were written to the repository (`git status` clean, none of the files it referenced exist
anywhere on disk). **Given the model's own tool execution was broken throughout, there is no
basis to trust that any of its reported numbers were genuinely computed rather than estimated or
confabulated under sandbox-failure pressure.** Per Rule 11/8c, none of these specific numbers are
recorded as findings. This is worth naming plainly as a real failure mode distinct from a wrong
derivation: a model reporting confident, specific-looking numerical output when its own tools
demonstrably did not run.

## An independent, real search: no improvement found (bounded, not conclusive)

Rather than rely on the above, built and ran an actual search myself, using a correctly-validated
implementation of Karp's maximum-mean-cycle algorithm (`/home/rat/.claude/jobs/a8d2d60e/tmp/karp_*.py`).
**Sanity check first**: applied the algorithm to the already-verified k=3 (mod 27) certificate from
round 19 and got exactly `rho=2.0`, matching the by-hand-verified value -- confirms the
implementation itself is correct before using it to search anything new.

Extended the k=3 policy to k=4 (mod 81) and k=5 (mod 243) by lookup on the low digits (`d(s) :=
d_table27[s mod 27]`): trivially still valid, still exactly `rho=2.0` at both window sizes (having
more visible context doesn't hurt, but doesn't help unless actively exploited -- expected, not a
finding on its own).

Ran a real, time-budgeted hill-climbing local search (start from the k=3-seeded policy, repeatedly
try alternate legal `d` choices per state, keep any strict improvement in the Karp-computed
worst-case `rho`), 40 seconds of actual search time each:

```
k=4 (n=54):  start rho=2.0, final rho=2.0, 6523 iterations tried, no improvement found
k=5 (n=162): start rho=2.0, final rho=2.0, 726 iterations tried, no improvement found
```

**No improvement below `rho=2` was found at either window size.** This is a real, honest negative
result, not proof: hill-climbing from one seed can get stuck in a local optimum, and k=5's search
was an order of magnitude less thorough (726 vs 6523 iterations) given the larger state space and
Karp's `O(V^3)`-ish per-evaluation cost, so it carries less weight. A proper resolution would need
either a much longer/smarter search (simulated annealing, multiple structurally-different seeds, or
a real mean-payoff-game solver rather than naive hill-climbing) or an analytic argument for why
`rho=2` might be optimal for bounded-memory cylinder policies specifically.

## Status

`j*(l)<=2l` (round 19) stands, unimproved. No evidence found, from either Codex's untrustworthy
report or my own bounded independent search, that a larger cylinder window pushes the bound below
2. This does not resolve whether `rho*` (the true limit as window size grows) equals `log_4(3)` or
sits strictly above it -- the search depth here is too shallow to distinguish those. If this
direction is pursued further, a properly implemented mean-payoff-game solver (value iteration or
policy iteration, not ad hoc hill-climbing) applied at larger `k` is the honest next step, not
another AI consultation without independent verification capacity to match.

# 2026-07-29 (twenty-first round): the honest next step, done -- a real mean-payoff-game
# solver. rho_4 = 5/3 exactly (leading constant 2 -> 5/3), and the window-k optima decrease
# toward (apparently) log_4(3)

Round 20 ended by naming the correct next step: stop hill-climbing, implement a real
mean-payoff-game (MPG) solver. Done this round. All code and a full README are committed under
`experiments/E-003-mpg-cylinder/`. Every number below is an exact rational carrying a matching
min-max saddle certificate (achievable-policy upper bound == adversary-committed lower bound), and
each was cross-validated by at least one fully independent method before being recorded.

## The object, re-derived from first principles (not copied)

Round 19's reformulation: state `z` (a 3-adic unit), legal cost `d>=0` iff `2^d z == 1 (mod 3)`,
transition `T_d(z) = (2^{d+1} z - 2)/3`; `j*(l) = max_z min-policy sum(d_i)` over l steps, modulus
shrinking one power of 3 per step. I re-implemented this from the stated definitions and, as an
independent grounding stronger than round 19's, checked the game DP not only against the known
table (extended to l=1..12, four points past round 19's l=10) but against a **direct brute-force
computation of `j*(l)` from the `R_{J-1,J}` covering definition** itself (l=1..6): all three agree
exactly (`step_a_grounding.py`). The game genuinely equals `j*(l)`; this is not taken on round 19's
word.

**The cylinder relaxation is a mean-payoff game, exactly.** Fix a window size `k`. A policy that
sees only `z mod 3^k` cannot know the next 3-adic digit; computing `T_d(z) mod 3^k` needs
`z mod 3^{k+1}`, i.e. one digit more than the window gives. So the successor
`s' = T_d(s + 3^k e) mod 3^k` depends on a hidden digit `e in {0,1,2}`. Model `e` as an adversary
maximizing the long-run average cost: the minimizer picks `d` from `s = z mod 3^k` (cost `d`), the
adversary picks `e`. The game value `rho_k` is the best asymptotic worst-case rate any window-k
cylinder policy can guarantee. Two facts make it the right object:
- **Window monotonicity (proven):** a window-k policy is a special window-(k+1) policy, so
  `rho_{k+1} <= rho_k`.
- **It upper-bounds the real rate (proven):** the optimal window-k policy carries a potential `h`
  with `d(s) + h[s'(e)] <= rho_k + h[s]` for every state and *every* hidden digit `e`
  (`certificate.py` verifies this, zero violations, k=3..9). Telescoping over l steps gives
  `j*(l) <= rho_k * l + C_k`, `C_k = max h - min h`. Hence `limsup_l j*(l)/l <= rho_k` for every
  `k`, hence `limsup_l j*(l)/l <= inf_k rho_k`, and `log_4(3) <= limsup_l j*(l)/l` (round 16's
  lower bound). **Correction, 2026-07-30 (round 24's A3 investigation, see that section)**: only
  this one direction (`limsup <= inf_k rho_k`) is proven here; the reverse inequality, and hence
  the equality `inf_k rho_k = limsup_l j*(l)/l` stated in earlier drafts of this note, was
  asserted, not proven. Round 24 found evidence (not proof) the window relaxation may have an
  irreducible gap (`inf_k rho_k > limsup_l j*(l)/l` genuinely possible), which would mean the
  `rho_k -> log_4(3)` extrapolation below is reading a different limit than it appears to.
  Computing `rho_k` is computing a convergent sequence of upper bounds on the exact asymptotic
  rate of `j*(l)/l`, which is H-003's central object.

## The solver and the result

`mpg4.py`: nested Howard strategy improvement -- the minimizer improves its positional policy;
each policy is evaluated by the adversary's exact max-mean-cycle best response (Howard, O(n)
memory, needed because the machine's RAM is committed to the l=24 run so Karp's O(n^2) is
unaffordable past k=7). At convergence the achievable value equals an adversary-committed lower
bound, a matching saddle certificate = an exact proof of the game value.

Exact, proven window-k optima:

```
k:      3    4     5    6    7     8      9     10
rho_k:  2   5/3   3/2  3/2  7/5  25/19  5/4   11/9
dec:  2.000 1.667 1.500 1.500 1.400 1.316 1.250 1.222
C_k:    5   19/3  15/2  9    10  207/19 47/4   --
```

Non-increasing, as the monotonicity theorem requires. Headline:

**`rho_4 = 5/3` exactly. This improves the leading constant of the best proven upper bound on
`j*(l)` from 2 (round 19) to 5/3: `j*(l) <= (5/3) l + O(1)`, and 5/3 is exactly optimal for a
window-4 cylinder policy (cannot be beaten without more window).** The sequence continues down to
`11/9 ~ 1.222` at k=10. Round 19 had found a single window-3 policy achieving `2l`; this round
proves `rho_3 = 2` is exactly optimal for window 3 *and* that wider windows do provably better.

## Verification (four independent ways, matching rounds 19-20's standard)

1. **Matching saddle certificate at every k** (upper == lower), self-certifying exactness.
2. **Reproduces the Karp-based solver** (`mpg.py`, an entirely separate value-iteration + Karp
   max/min-mean-cycle code path): rho_3=2, rho_4=5/3, rho_5=3/2 all match.
3. **Independent long value iteration** (raw min-max Bellman operator, no Howard, no Karp): k=7
   gives `max_s V_8000/8000 = 1.40025 -> 7/5`, matching.
4. **dcap-robustness:** rho_7=7/5 and rho_8=25/19 are unchanged across action caps 20/40/80, and
   the largest immediate cost the optimal policy ever uses is <=9, far below the cap, so the value
   is not an artifact of truncating the (infinite) action set.
5. **Ran the optimal policies on the actual finite covering DP** (`verify_on_real.py`,
   round-19-style): for k=4 and k=7, over every unit residue mod 3^l, l up to 14, **zero
   failures** (never an illegal move or a non-unit state), worst-case cost = `rho_k * l + C_k`
   with `C_k` growing with `k` (so a wider window only wins at larger l; at fixed small l a narrow
   window is better, exactly as the additive constants predict).

## Where is `rho_k` heading? Suggestive, not decisive.

`limsup_l j*(l)/l <= inf_k rho_k` (only this direction proven, see the correction above). At k=10 `rho_k = 1.222`, still far above `log_4(3) = 0.79248` and
decreasing slowly -- the same qualitative picture as the *actual* `j*(l)/l` data, which runs
`j*(15)/15 = 1.333` down to `j*(23)/23 = 1.174` and is likewise nowhere near 0.79 at accessible
sizes. Fitting `rho_k = L + A/k` to the trailing points gives `L = 0.783` (k>=6) and `L = 0.788`
(k>=7), both essentially `log_4(3)`. A full-data `L + A/k^p` fit is looser (`p=1.16`, `L~1.0`). So
the data is **consistent with `rho_k -> log_4(3)`** -- which, if it held, would give
`limsup j*(l)/l = log_4(3)` and hence `e(l) = o(l)`, ruling out the slow-linear branch of E-002's
question -- but an 8-point extrapolation over sticky rational values cannot decide between a limit
of `~0.79` and one anywhere up to `~1.0`. This does **not** resolve log-vs-sqrt-vs-linear. It is,
though, the first evidence from an independent construction (cylinder MPG values) pointing at the
same `log_4(3)` rate, and it is mutually consistent with the primary `j*(l)` data.

## Two corrections to round 20, recorded per Rule 8c

- **Round 20's naive hill-climbing was genuinely too weak, not just inconclusive.** It found
  nothing below `rho=2` at k=4 after 6523 iterations; the true window-4 optimum is `5/3`, which a
  correct MPG solver finds immediately. The task's premise (proper solver >> hill-climbing) was
  right.
- **Codex's round-20 formula `rho_k = 1 + 2/(k-1)` is false.** It matches k=3,4,5 (2, 5/3, 3/2)
  and, by coincidence, k=9 (5/4), but is wrong at k=6 (predicts 7/5, true 3/2), k=7 (predicts 4/3,
  true 7/5), and k=8 (predicts 9/7, true 25/19). Round 20 was right to distrust it as confabulated
  under tool failure; the partial match was luck, not a real result.

## Confidence

**High** on the exact `rho_k` for k=3..10: self-certifying, cross-validated four independent ways,
robust, and consistent with all prior ground truth. `rho_4 = 5/3` as a proven improvement of the
leading constant from 2 to 5/3 is solid and citable (labeled, per Rule 10b, as a proven upper
bound on `j*(l)`, not a statement about `e(l)`). **Low-to-medium** on the limit interpretation: the
`log_4(3)` extrapolation is suggestive and consistent but model-dependent and not a proof; it must
not be reported as resolving `e(l)`'s growth. (k=11, n=118098, was left running in the background
in pure Python; it will add one trailing point but is not needed for any claim above.)

## Independent spot-check, same day: `rho_4` and `rho_5` re-confirmed, one script bug found and
## resolved (not the result)

Did not accept the round on the strength of its own five self-checks alone. Re-ran the committed
code myself: `mpg.py 3 4 5` reproduced `rho_3=2`, `rho_4=5/3` with a matching min-policy/adversary
certificate both times (exact match with the round's own numbers). At `k=5`, `mpg.py`'s own
adversary-lower-bound routine returned `1` against a min-policy upper bound of `3/2` -- a genuine
certificate mismatch, not resolved by 5x more value-iteration steps (6000 vs 1200), pointing to a
bug isolated to that one auxiliary cross-check function rather than non-convergence.

Resolved by going to ground truth rather than trusting either script: ran `verify_on_real.py`'s
actual finite covering DP (the same style as rounds 19-20) with the `k=5`-optimal policy over
every unit residue mod `3^l`, `l=5..14`. **Zero failures at every `l`**, and the achieved
worst-case cost divided by `l` visibly trends downward toward `1.5` as `l` grows (2.0, 2.0, 1.857,
1.875, 1.778, 1.8, 1.727, 1.75, 1.692, 1.714 for `l=5..14`), consistent with `rho_5*l+O(1)`. This
is real, achievable, ground-truth confirmation of `rho_5=3/2`, independent of the buggy auxiliary
script. The bug in `mpg.py`'s standalone adversary-bound routine is noted for any future reuse of
that file but does not affect `mpg4.py` (the main solver, which reports and independently
cross-checks a matching Karp certificate for every `k`) or any number in this round's table.

## Two follow-ups the same day: a new data point, and a novelty check

**`rho_11 = 6/5`, tight, `n=118098`.** A background sweep from round 21, left running
unattended outside this repo, had already computed this before being found (already at
1.8GB RSS, about to start `k=12` at 3x the state space, a real memory risk next to the
concurrent l=24 run) and killed. Recorded in `experiments/E-003-mpg-cylinder/README.md`.

**Novelty check, before any claim this technique itself is new.** Searched for prior
art on "mean-payoff games" applied to covering-type problems. Found real, close
precedent: Meyerovitch & Young, "Rationality and computability of the covering radius
for sofic shifts" (arXiv:2603.21449, 2026, L-062), which proves rationality and
computability of a covering-radius quantity for sofic shifts via a direct variant of
Ehrenfeucht-Mycielski's 1979 mean-payoff games -- the same general technique, a
different object (symbolic dynamics / coding theory, not this project's number-theoretic
`j*(l)`). **The mean-payoff-game framing itself is not a new contribution; the
application to Wirsching's covering problem is.** This matters directly for how round
21 should ever be described in a paper: as an application of an established technique
to a new problem, not as a new method, per Rule 10b (label results by what they
actually are).
